         (Cont'd from Page 11 111 )
        ••••••••• BECOMING DIMMER as the evening hours pass by. Some people
        have reported that they feel this star jumps or vibrates slightly. Ther(
        is nothing significant about any of these sightings further t o the above .
        On each incident that a call was recei ved at this Detachment, same vas
        checked out.
        It,      -         (Mrs.                              Deer Lake! Nt'ld.,
        a licenced astronomer who                  rvatory was contacted. She
        states there is no doubt that the object described is the planet, Venus.
        She has been attracted to this planet of recent due to its brilllance.
        She advises that 1t was and will be the brightest star in the sky until
        early spring of 1969. She states that the planet Venus is the most
        visible star and that it can be seen each evening from sun-set until
                                      1•
        about mid-night and that it gradually drifts down below the horizon.
        She explained that it     brighter during the early hours of each evening
        because at that time it is more overhead and there is much less visual
        obstruction in the atmosphere. Also as it drifts away to the West and
        downwards to the horizon, we see it lhrough heavy atmospheric conditions
        (haze - clouds - smoke -etc.). This condition has been~ of
        recent dates due to our cloudy weather conditions . Mrs. llllllllllstatee
        that Venus appears to jump or vibrate when the density of atmospheric
        pressure is high . This is a mere optical illusion caused by the
        atmospheric conditions.
        5.       In view of the foregoing, I do not feel the sightings as
      · reported in this regard can be classified as Meteors, Fireballs, or
        Unidentified Flying Objects. The incident of 30 DEC 68 being the sun-
        set rays shining on the vapor left by a jet aircraft and subsequent
        reports of the asteroid object in the Western sky being the planet Venus.
        6.           The f oregoing is being submitted tor information purposes only .

         5.        At 12:20 A.M..           came to the office and stated
         that he could see another obje    very similar to the first. This
         time it was situated directly behind the office. I observed
         the object along with Cat . MADRIOGA. At this time, we could see
         an object that was turning all colours, (i.e. from red through·~ to
         blue).
         6.         D.O.T. Meteorological Office was contacted and they
         advised that the atmosphere was full of ice crystals. At this
         time, MADRIGQA stated that be had read an article about
         stars that turned all colours. He further advised that this
         was the result of ice crystals in the air acting as prisms. The
         light from the stars goea through the ice crystal and as a
         result the white light from the stars comes out as a spectrum
         or colour.
         1.         A further check was made to see if we could still see
         the object and at this time several stars were seen to be
         changing colours at the same time. We have come to a conclusion
         that the 'O.F.o.• was a star changing colours as a result of
         the ice crystals in the atmosphere. No further action taken at
         this point .

               All indications are that this was the object that these persona
               observed. It bad been witnessed by the complainant and his
               friends and they are all agreeable that the object they bad seen
               on the previous evening was the same as the one they were presently
               watching.
               5:      In view of the faot this object has been identified and
               its~ re-occurrence the following evening and after having being
               further witnessed by the complainant and his friends during the
               same time and area it is felt that this was quite a natural
               cccurrence and no further investigation is deemed necessary.'
               All the subjects who were intervi ewed are satisfied that this
               object ns in :tact the Evening Star of Venue after having viewed
               it on two succassive evenings;

               CONCLIJDED HERE1

   1.     The following report is being submitted in compliance with exiat-
   ing instructions as outlined in Operational Manual CO- Alr , Unidentified
   Flying Objects.
                                      23 NOV 68
                         AMi C.s.T., t his date, I was proceeding s outh on #4 Prov.
            ~pp r'o~Lm~~e y   6 miles south of ~Sask . , via private auto.
                          of l~eadow Lake and a ~ for the Sask. Power Cor p .
            pa:ss.:tng.er im my vehicle . ','fe were en route to the Meadow Lake Game
                to go moose hunting. Weather conditions at the time were cold
                clear , the sun was just appeari ng in the south-easte rn sky and
                were visible in the sky at this time .
  ).       ·~en at the above noted location , we observed a bri6ht object sligh~
  ly larger than a star diractly west of our location. The object appeared
  to be travellin6 in a southerly direction just above the western horizon.
  From our position on tha hi.,.hway , i t appeared to be over the loiakwa Dist .,
  Sask . I stopped my car to observe this li6ht. The light which resembled a
  star only slightlY larger as I have statdd seemed to hover over the Makwa
  Dist . , for a few moments . It than proceeded in a northerly direction for
  a.relativa short distance, stopped and then proc~eded south for a short
  distance past the point where it originally hovered . The object repeated
  this routine approximately four times . It may be mentioned tbat the object
  shed the same light as a star only it was noticeably brig~ter. During the
  above mentioned actions, it grew :noticeably briehter and would slowly fade .
  This change in light i ntensity occurred aporoximately four times. The obje-
  ct returned to the point where it originally hovered and then appeared to
  travel straight up for a short distance and then returned to its starting
  point . It then travelled str aight south and was out out of sight in a
  matter of seconds , I t may be mentioned that when thg object started south
  the acceleration was instantaneous and the speed was incredible . This whola

                          Sichting of Unidentified Flyinf Object
                          Fort Qu'Appel le Diet., Sask. 2 NOV 68


                                                  2l NOV 6S                •

        1. At ~:30 A.M. on the noted date the writer was on patrol five (5)
        miles west, southwest, ot Fort Qu'Appelle, Sask. At this point I
        was proceeding north on # 210 Highway when a small bright white light
        appeared over the highway an estimated distance of )/8 of a mile in
        front or to the north of my position. I proceeded on north toward
        this light, which was approxillately fi.Cteen (1.5) ieet above the ground,
        and thi.s l1ght began movinc to the east. The police car was accelerated
        and this light also began •oving at a higher speed due east. At a point
        on the highway over which the light was first observed the police oar
        was stopped and the light could still be observedL now stationary,
        over soma bushes approximately ! mile due east. The light then began
        moving very slowly to the south and I began backing the police oar
        south alao. After it had travelled a dist ance of approximately one
        hundred (100) yards it suddenly want straight up at a very high rate
        of apeed and at an estiaated distance of 150 feet above the g.r ound
        the light went out and nothing mora was seen.
        2. At the time of the sighting the SkY was clear but there was
        no moonlight. No object was observed around the light due totbe
        darkness and the distance from the observor. It was in the sight of the
        writer for a period of about ~5 seconds. I was alone at the time. No
        sound was heard.
        ) . Later this same date a check was made on the ground at the point
        ol sighting but nothing unusual was observed. There waa no one else
        in the area at the time and the light was not observed by anyone else.
        ~.       Extra copies of this report attached.

      2.      Further to the above noted per submitted by Humboldt Detachment,
      to which this report corresponds, the following information is being
      submitted by this detachment in compliance with Operational Manual CO- air.
                         Date and time of sighting: 9:30 PM.~ 2 NOV 68
                         Condition of sky: Clear
                         Location of observer: Standing outside the Chi ck- En-Lick
                         Drive- in Restaurant in the town of Lanigan, Sask.
                         Occurrence o! bursts: None
                         Luminosity: Extremely bright, brighter than moon and planets,
                         lighted the sky 1n area of path, no nearby objects thus no
                         shadows casted .
                 (f)     Colour : fireball first very bright white with a short whi te
                         tail and as it plunged to the earth the ball and tail changed
                         to a bright orange-red in colour with bright orange sparks
                         falling off the tail . The tail became much longer as it fell .
                 (g)     Form or shape: Round and approx. half the size of the full
                         moon. One tail or vapour trail which became longer as it fell
                         to the ear th .
                         Duration: Lasted approx. five seconds .
                         Sound: ~lone heard.

                         height could not be estimated) fell in a path to the southwest
                         direction at a 4.5 degree angle .

                                 Re:         U. F. O. Found in Northern Saskatchewan
                                            ------ -- - - - - --- - --- -
            1.         For warded herevith for your infor mati on is a copy of
            our r eport to Mr. Hodges respecting a sheet or met al which he
            asked us to analyse . Also enclosed is a set or photogr aphs
            t aken by our Photographic Sect i on while the item was in the
            Laboratory f or examination.
            2.         We hav e been advised by Mr . Rodges that this material
            vas found by a helicopter pilot working 1n the Wollaston Lake
            area flying in equipment to a hard rook diamond drilling outfit .
            The pilot who was a Regina resident was aware of Mr. Hodges •
            work and i nterest in meteorites a nd the upper atmosphere and
            submitted the material to him for the purpose of i dentifjcati on.
            At differ ent times it has been t hought to bea (A) a part of
            an aircra:t't , (B) a nose cone of a miss ile and , (C) a segment
            of space hardware , probablY a portion of the actual s~in of
            a sat ellite. This Laboratory ' s analysis of t he metal also
            disclosed it to be a high purity titanium and has arguments
            against the possibility that it is a portion of a.n a.iroraft .
            Whi le it was fir s t thought that the pat tern of radial and
            concentric lines on the 11 s1licone" side of the sheet vas the
            outline of a solar cell battery system , such as is mounted
            on satellites, our l aboratory examination has established that
            this must have been the inner s ide of the ma t erial and must
            therefore be related to some type of skeletal structure . The
            other sid e of the sheet , which bears a ceramic type of coating
            is ther efor e likely to have been an external surface designed
            t o withstand the higher temperatures which high velocity
            through the atmosphere may produce.

~   0 NF X DE NT I A L
                     Re:   U.F.O . Found in Northern Saskatchewan
                           --------------------
 3·         Dr. Ian Halliday of the Dominion Observatory, Ottawa,
 Dr. Peter M. Milliman , Chief of Upper Atmosphere Research ,
 National Research Oounc1l, Ottawa and Hr. Joha DoUglas of the
 Department of Energy , Mines and Surveys, Ottawa, who is also
 a member of the Associate Committee on Upper Atmospher e have
 all been made aware of tne recovery of this material and are
 studying the matter from th.eir respective viewpoints . Tests
 were conducted by members of the Provincial Government for
 r adioactivity wllich , we are advised , simply indicated that the
 material had been down for more than 90 days . While the
 material was in the Laboratory custody through the Commanding
 Officer of the Ca~dian Forces Base at Moose Ja w, Colonel
 Larry Kieland, arrangements were made for two of his officers
 acquainted with missiles and high altitude aircraft design
 to view the item and suggest possible sources for it. Major
 Sutherland and Lietenant Gray were confident that it did not
 correspond "''ith any structural components of an aircraft either
 military or civilian on which titanium was currently be~ng used.
 They were equally coni'ident that it did not correspond in
 design to any material which mi ght be involved in the design
 of missiles of the types presently located on Canadian soil
 a nd such others in foreign service with which they were
 acquainted.
 4.         Mr. Hodges advises that three possibilit i es still
 exist' (A) that i t may be a part of a "Courier" satellite
 placed in a near circumpolar orbit for military reconnaissance
 purposes and launched fl;'om Va.n denberg Air Base, U.S .A.,
 (B) that i t is a segment af a satellite placed in near
 circumpolar orbit l aunched from some U. S.S. R. l ocatian,
 (C) that it is a portion of the protective jacket in which the
 Canadian Satellites "AJ.J.ouette I and II" were launched.
 Mr. Hodges also advises that 1f the identity of this material
 can be established as a satellite component it will constitute
 the largest element yet recovered on this continent and pernaps
 in the world. In this r espect it ~ght be noted that the
 present theory supporting the proposition that the metal is
 satellite material argues that it survived the usually fatal
 conditions of re-entry due to the fact that in separation
 from the rest of the structure it acquired a formation which
 happened to have aerodynamic qualities. These qualities enabled
 it to flutter or fly to earth at a gr eatly reduced velocity
 thereby avoiding the temperatures which initially had commenced
 t0 consume it.

 this material personally to the National Research Council in
 Ottawa for further study .
 6.        The Commanding Officer "F" Division is being kept
 informed of this Laboratory ' s par ticipation in the matter.

                       - un- siliconed, radial bands
                       - un-siliconed concentric bands

                            bands .

                            edge .

Note A:      For want of a more convenient means of orientation the
             following terms have been used in the explanation of the
             photographs :
                                - 3 -
            silicone side - that side of the sheet which bears
            deposits of green powder which spectrographic analysis
            disclosed to be primarily silicates. It also bears the
            image of the structure to which the sheet was attached.
            ceramic side - that side of the s heet whi ch bears a
            grayish coloured coating which spectrographic analysis
            disclosed to be primarily aluminum oxide .
            leadinB &dge - that edge of the sheet which bears
            evidence of burning . It might otherwise have been
            described as the top and right end , as viewed from
            the silicone side with longest dimension down .
             trailing edg~ - that edge of the sheet which bears no
             evidence ofurning , and which is its l ongest dimension.
Note a,     (See photo #29) s   If collar vas positioned flush against
                                face of sheet, flang e on underside of
                                collar would provide an abutment
                                against which the ends of the framework
                                or honeycomb s truc ture would rest .
                                Sheet might then be fixed in position
                                with respect to framework by rivets
                                extending from collar, t hrough thin
                                member of framework and through sheet.
Note C;     The ceramic material evidently conc ealed all rivets in
            sheet as the coating has been ruptured wherever rivets
            have 1oeen forcibly removed. There is no evidence of
            any intentional break, or opening in the sheet which
            might serve to accommodate electrical circuitry, or
            provide access from one side to t he other .
Note D'     The contour of the sheet suggests that it was designed
            to have the shape of a frustrum of a cone, having a slant
            height of slightly over 25" (2ltt" from top rivet to
            bottom rivet) and a vertex about 61" from the "trailing"
            or bo ttom edge .
No t e EJ   The shape of the collar and the evidence existi ng of
            the sheet and the collar having sandwiched projections
            of whatever structure was responsible tor t he radial
            and concentric bands leads to the conclusion tha t the
            silicone side was the inner or underside of the cone.
            The ceramic side t hen evidently constituted a smooth
            outer surface without interruption , at least insofar as
            this area was concerned.
Note F:     The last r ow of rivet holes along the "trailing'' edge
            of the sheet are slightly larger than others a nd all
            appear concave or sauoered vhen viewed from the ceramic
            side indicati ve of the sheet having been pulled from
            the lramework to which the silicone side was strapped •

                                                                    .... .
..• . -
   ,


                                     - 4-

   Note Gs   In the area along the "leading" edge Which displays pro-
             nounced evidel;lce oi' burning, no remnants, molten or
             otherwise! were found of any other structural components .
             This woula indi cate that the sheet became separated
             from the structure to which it had been r1vetted prior
             to being subjected to the heat which commenced to consume
             it.

                                     The CO Labs . telephoned this afternoon and
                       advised that he had received the following information
                       from Insp . Huber of tbe Regina Lab ,
                       2,                  f-Ir. John Hodges , the Regional Repr eaentati ve
                       for the National Research Council Associate Comnittee on
                       Meteorites had received from a helicopter pilot an object
                       thought to be a meteorite and had brought this to our Lab .
                       for examination . This substance has proved to be
                       TITANIUI4 , 99% pure, which is thow~:ht to be the largest
                       piece from a sat6nite tmt has ever landed on earth. It
                       is 4 ft . long, 2~ ft . wide and l'leighs between 10 and 15
                       pounds . It is understood that there is enough material
                       to make positive identi fication and Hodges is trying to
                       determine if this is from a ll . S . or a Russian satellite .
                       A Dr , Jo'oran of Har vard University is also assisting to
                       attempt to identify the orbit from which this fell .       It is
                       understood that ~!r . Ian Halliday , Chief, Dominion
                       Observatory, is also in possession of this information .
                       J,             The CO Labs . advised that the local newspaper ,
                       The Leader Post , is aware of this find, however is holding
                       up any news release until more identification can be made
                       of tho material and apparently the Press does know t hat our
                       Lab . participated.

                   It was oomiDg from a north-easterly direction, It was a blink.i ng
                   red and green light. As it cot closer the red light w•nt out
                   and there waa no more blinking, Then there was a li&ht coloured
                   amber light below and behind the green light. There waa little
                   noise, it was like the noise heard from a oar motor with a
                   muffler. These lights were at an altitude of approximately
                   1,500 feet. We watched these li&hte until they disappeared,
                   It was mortnc Yer'f taat. lie watched the. lights for 2 or 3
                   ainutea. The lights disappeared aouth ot ua. The red ligbt
                   neYer came on again.
